In autumn 1783, in a Kyoto merchant townhouse on Shijo-dori, during the hour between lamp-lighting and full dark, Mieko, a Japanese woman of thirty-two with a long oval face, narrow eyes, and hair dressed in a mature shimada style with dark horn pins, wears her moss-green komon with scattered maple leaves, the robe loosened at her collar so that her right shoulder and the upper curve of her breast press against the screen's edge as she laughs; beside her, Hanae, a Japanese woman of twenty-eight with a broader jaw, fuller lips, and hair in a lower take-hyogo knot with a single tortoiseshell comb, wears her persimmon kosode partly displaced and her dove-grey haori fallen open, her exposed neck flushed with one small beni blush as their gazes meet with shared amusement. A pet cricket has escaped its woven bamboo cage visible in the lower prop strip at image-left, its chirp now audible from beneath the folding screen whose painted willow branches tilt precariously into the central band; the sparse upper strip shows shoji panels with bokashi shifting from deep ai indigo to warm parchment, willow branch shadows keyed in dark contour against the washi. Dominant flat silhouettes emerge from Mieko's dark hair mass and extended reaching arm, Hanae's angled torso and clutching hand, the folding screen's diagonal lean, and the cricket cage's cylindrical form; crisp overlap places Mieko's reaching arm beneath the screen frame, Hanae's haori sleeve across her own knee, and negative space pools between their faces where eye lines converge. The palette anchors on ai indigo and persimmon with beni red on maple motifs, moss green and dove grey as flat separated fields, warm unbleached-washi skin base with one bounded beni blush on cheeks and neck; the bokashi gradient is confined to the upper shoji strip only, shifting indigo to parchment without touching the central tableau. Close-read performer cues include Mieko's mature oval jawline and visible hand ownership of her right fingers spreading beneath the screen's lower rail, plus Hanae's registered persimmon kosode pattern of small repeated fans aligned across her visible thigh; physical set craft shows smoke-darkened shoji frame edges, polished lacquer tray corners with fine scratches from daily tea service, and a threshold mat faded to paler indigo at its center from foot traffic while protected corners retain deeper color. On this summer festival morning in 1829, inside a Nagoya drum-maker's final tuning room, the younger woman aged twenty-six is a festival-season assistant with round mature face, short practical hair pinned severely back, persimmon-orange work kosode discarded to her waist with sleeves trailing, fundoshi loosened to mid-thigh, cheeks flushed persimmon with exertion and amusement, her gaze darting toward the elder's turned profile with startled mirth; the elder woman aged thirty-one is the drum-master's daughter with longer oval mature face, hair in a neat working knot with two dark pins, blue-grey cotton kosode with small drum-crest patterns fallen from both shoulders to bunch at her waist revealing bare back and loosened scarlet fundoshi, her own cheeks equally flushed as she twists to meet the younger's glance with shared exasperation and warmth. The comic interruption appears in the lower strip where the mallet head has struck a ceramic water jar's rim sending a shallow arc of water across the tatami, while the central band holds both women's overlapping torsos against the great drum's broad circle, and the upper strip shows the tuning room's shadowed ceiling beams with one open shoji rectangle admitting summer morning ochre light and a glimpse of festival banners outside. Dominant flat silhouettes arise from the drum's perfect circle edge in dark sumi-grey, the younger woman's persimmon waist kosode falling in cascading folds, the elder's blue-grey cotton bunching at her hips, and their overlapping torso-back contour where breast meets shoulder blade; crisp overlap of the younger's right forearm across the elder's left side wall creates one diagonal from mallet handle lower-left to drum rim upper-right, while negative space pools in the drum's interior circle and the upper shoji rectangle. The comic interruption manifests as a small tanuki-shaped paper lantern in the upper architectural strip that seems to tilt and wink, having been knocked askew by the younger's flung hand, while the lower prop strip shows the now-completely unbound rush mat with its tie coiled like a playful snake beside a wooden measuring stick; the central tableau fills with their reclined body pressure and the younger's pointing gesture. Summer 1792, Gifu countryside farmhouse veranda, deep evening hour, two Japanese women aged thirty-two and twenty-six: the elder with oval mature face, her low bun loosened so one strand escapes at her neck, her indigo millet-pattern yukata fallen completely from her shoulders to bunch at her waist, exposing her chest and the weathered tan of her forearms; the younger with rounder mature face, her side-swept knot partially unpinned so strands frame her cheeks, her white iris-round yukata open to the waist and slipping from her right hip, both faces turned full toward each other with eyes closed in the kiss, the elder's thumbs visible at the cousin's jawline, the younger's fingers white-knuckled in indigo shoulder fabric and her own thigh-gathered white cloth, their bent heads creating one shared silhouette at image-center. The fireflies now scattered across all three bands, their pale green motes descending like floating punctuation; the lower prop strip shows the abandoned split cage on its side at image-right, a few iris petals crushed where the younger woman's ankle crossed, millet grains scattered from the elder's knee-worn hem now fallen at center; the upper strip shows the eave's dark timber and the same lantern, now upright and steady, its warm ochre a calm witness to their absorbed kiss. Their intertwined bodies form a compact knot at center, the dominant vertical of the veranda post behind the younger woman's back crossed by the diagonal thrust of the elder's forward-leaning torso; negative space opens at image-right where the cage lies abandoned and fireflies drift across emptiness, while the overlapping indigo and white fabrics create a rhythmic pulse of keyblock contour against the warm wood plane. Four-color Edo pigment hierarchy: ai indigo anchors the elder's bunched yukata, the veranda post, and upper eave shadow; pale unbleached-washi grounds the younger's white yukata, both skins, and the wood surface; beni-vermilion warms their shared lip contact, the lantern square, and one small bounded blush at the younger's throat where the elder's thumb presses; violet iris accent pulses in the younger's scattered round motifs and the single crushed stem in the lower strip; firefly pale green drifts across all bands as the only element breaking horizontal containment; one bokashi gradient deepens from warm ochre at the lantern toward indigo shadow at the eave's left corner, strictly in the upper strip. Close performer coherence emerges through the younger's round mature jaw shape visible between the elder's cupping thumbs, and the registered violet iris rounds of her yukata now compressed and twisted at her gathered thigh; the elder's weathered forearm contour and the visible side-seam of her indigo yukata where it has slipped to expose her waist, the millet pattern registered across the folded bunched fabric. Physical set craft shows blind-embossed paper texture in the upright lantern panel; three patina cues: the veranda wood slightly silvered and darkened at the post base where the younger woman's back presses a polished hollow, the elder's indigo cotton with millet-pattern wear elongated at her left forearm from habitual washhouse wringing, and the younger's white yukata with localized yellowing at its inner collar from successive summers of humidity and sun. A torn folding screen dominates the central band, its fresh washi wrinkled into comic accordion folds where paste failed; the lower strip crops the worktable's glossy paste-contact center with brushes, paste bowl, and weighted paper scraps; the upper strip shows the workshop's open transom door with sudden draft lifting the screen's loose corner in a white flap against warm morning ochre, completing the three-part joke of human error, material rebellion, and atmospheric mischief. Dominant flat silhouettes emerge from Hana's dark mill-brown mass and Tama's pale blue-grey form pressed together in a back-to-front overlap, separated by the wrinkled paper's white zigzag contour and the dangling brush's diagonal slash; both women's heads turn toward each other along one rising diagonal from image-left lower to image-right upper, their parallel forearms and the screen's vertical frame creating a rhythmic counter-diagonal, the negative space of morning door ochre activating the upper right quadrant. The four-to-six-color Edo pigment hierarchy anchors on ai-indigo dark brown with sumi-black contour lines, pale unbleached-washi cream for paste and exposed skin, beni-red cherry accent in Tama's scattered petal rounds and one small bounded blush at both women's exerted cheeks plus Tama's exposed breast, and malachite-bright tool handles in the lower strip; one bokashi gradient deepens from workshop shadow brown to warm door ochre strictly across the upper transom strip, while all skin remains warm matte parchment with crisp contour edges and no interior modeling. Two close-read performer cues confirm live adults: Hana's mature broad-cheeked face shape distinct from Tama's sharper jaw and visible laugh-catch at the mouth corner, plus the apprentice's visible left hand owning its spread-fingered gesture landing on Hana's waist with paste-whitened fingertips matching her sleeve smears; the brown tsumugi's mill-pattern registration shows slight shift at the right forearm's worn zone from habitual brush motion, and the worktable wood holds glossy translucency at paste-contact center from years of sizing while blue-grey cotton stiffens with dried paste at Tama's left sleeve from previous repairs. Edo-period material patina appears only as bounded wear on physical costumes, props, and set surfaces. It is late spring 1769, the hour of the serpent, in a Shinano sericulture tray room; the thirty-four-year-old Japanese woman, a silk grower's widow, has a composed oblong face with slightly hooded eyes, her black hair in a practical age-appropriate marumage with lacquer pins, wearing a deep moss-green tsumugi work kosode whose scattered silkworm patterns are visible across her back and fallen open at the front to expose a pale under-robe, her expression caught between exasperation and reluctant amusement as she meets the younger woman's gaze over her shoulder; the twenty-nine-year-old Japanese woman, her spring niece-apprentice, has a rounder face with wider-set eyes and a small mole at the jaw, her hair in a simpler shimada variant with fewer ornaments, wearing a pale saffron kosode with bold mulberry-leaf rounds that has slipped its left shoulder tie and gapes open across her chest and belly, her mouth open in breathless laughter, her eye line directed up to the elder's face in shared recognition rather than apology. The comic interruption of the sticking frame's sudden release sends mulberry-leaf trays swaying in the upper strip, one tilted tray spilling a few leaves into the lower prop strip where a single mature silkworm spins a visible thread upward between the women's shoulders; the central band compresses both bodies against the wall between rack silhouettes, while the upper strip shows the lattice vent with morning ochre bokashi gradation from deep moss shadow. Dominant flat silhouettes arise from the vertical swaying rack frames, the horizontal tilted trays, the elder's broad back in dark moss-green, and the younger's angled torso in pale saffron; crisp overlap occurs where saffron sleeve crosses moss-green shoulder and where displaced obi sashes tangle at the wall junction; a rising diagonal from the extended back leg through the tilted tray organizes the compressed central band against negative space of pale wall. The palette holds four colors: deep ai-indigo anchor in rack shadows and hair masses; warm beni-red accent in mulberry motifs and the elder's under-robe glimpsed at her neckline; pale unbleached-washi ground in wall and vent light; and dominant moss-green and saffron pigment blocks in the two kosode, with skin as warm matte parchment carrying one small bounded moss blush at the elder's exerted cheek and the younger's exposed shoulder where silk has slipped. Close-read performer cues include the thirty-four-year-old's mature oblong face shape with slight nasolabial folds visible in three-quarter turn, and the visible seam of the younger's saffron kosode following her shoulder to wrist confirming the garment's physical construction rather than painted pattern; physical set craft shows woven tray-bottom texture and lacquered frame corners, with patina including tray wood polished smooth and slightly darkened at hand-grip rails from decades of sliding frames, the elder's moss-green tsumugi with silkworm-pattern wear and slight nap compression at the right forearm from habitual mulberry feeding, the younger's saffron silk with mulberry-pattern fade and localized yellowing at the back from repeated tray leaning against her spine, and bamboo wall slats smoke-darkened near the ceiling from years of hearth proximity. Dominant flat silhouettes emerge from the younger's cascading black hair mass, the elder's low knot and nape line, the torn stencil's angular cutout, the morning-glory vine sleeve spilling image-left, and the wave-pattern hip descending image-right; crisp overlap at their pressed backs creates a dark keyblock contour where indigo and persimmon meet, while a descending diagonal from the suspended brush through the stencil tear to the compressed books animates the static collapse. The six-color Edo palette assigns ai-indigo anchor to the elder's wave-pattern kosode and upper strip shadow, persimmon dominant to the younger's morning-glory kosode, beni-red accent to scattered morning-glory blossoms, warm cream ground to untorn stencil papers and the elder's exposed back, pale ochre vent light to the upper lattice strip, and one small malachite accent to the inkstone base; skin is warm matte parchment with one bounded indigo blush at the younger's exposed breast and both women's flustered cheeks; bokashi gradation moves from deep indigo at the pattern room's upper left corner through worked-air ochre to pale vent warmth at the upper right lattice only. Close-read performer cues include the younger's distinct heart-shaped jaw and the elder's visible left hand with its practiced brush-grip thumb position, plus three localized patina marks: the work surface glossy at center from years of resist-paste application creating a pale sheen zone, the elder's indigo right forearm with wave pattern abraded to lighter value from habitual tracing motion, and the younger's persimmon shoulder where morning-glory vines fade to cream from repeated pattern-table leaning. The complete image remains a flat, borderless, three-band nishiki-e theatrical picture plane; adult performers are recognizable only through mature faces, coherent joints, hand tasks, support, and contact, while every form stays within matte contour fields on one shallow picture surface.

It is the summer of 1838 in a Nagoya mikoshi builder's finishing shed at mid-morning; the elder woman, Japanese, thirty, with a broad mature jaw and small scar through left eyebrow, wears her brown crane-pattern tsumugi work coat fallen open at the chest to expose the collar of an indigo under-robe, her hair in a practical low bun with two broken ivory pins; the younger, Japanese, twenty-seven, with narrower mature cheekbones and freckles across her nose, wears her grey-green iris-pattern kosode with sleeves pushed to elbows and coat tails ridden up to expose her lower back and the full length of her lifted left thigh, her hair in a disheveled festival-season topknot with one long strand escaped; both women gaze at each other with slightly squinted laughing eyes and exposed upper teeth, the elder's visible hands white-knuckled on the pole, the apprentice's visible hands gripping the frame rail with spread fingers. The comic interruption manifests as the miniature shrine's visible sway: the lower strip shows the shrine's red lacquer base corner and one fallen festival bell, the central band captures the women's braced laughter and pressed bodies against the diagonal pole, and the upper strip shows the shed's open roof framing a bokashi gradient from deep shadow brown to warm door ochre where morning light enters and the shrine's tiny gilded roof ornament tilts askew; a second loose bell hangs from the miniature roof at the strip's edge.
